The D visa and TRP are different permissions; a D visa is not a multi-year study residence card.

1. Admission and eligible institution.

Obtain official admission/acceptance confirmation from an Estonian institution recognised by the Republic of Estonia. The institution supplies the invitation/confirmation used for the visa and residence process. For university and professional higher-education programmes, the student must demonstrate the required language ability for the language of instruction. Confirm the specific programme's admission, tuition-payment and language requirements directly with the university; admission alone does not guarantee a visa or TRP.

2. Sri Lankan submission route — critical distinction.

The Estonian Embassy in New Delhi's country-specific page states that Sri Lankan residents apply for Schengen short-stay C visas through the German Embassy in Colombo, but explicitly warns that the represented embassy cannot accept Estonian long-stay D visas. Sri Lankan D-visa and TRP applicants must book an appointment with the Estonian Embassy in New Delhi, which accepts these applications by appointment only. The booking must be made personally and is free; include the reason for the D visa/TRP and the pre-filled application number in the appointment comment. Do not send documents before the Embassy requests them. Verify the appointment and current jurisdiction with the Embassy before booking travel to India.

3. D visa — core documents and sequence.

Apply in person at the competent Estonian representation. The New Delhi checklist requires a valid passport issued within the previous 10 years, with at least two blank pages and validity at least three months beyond the visa expiry; completed and signed application; recent ICAO-compliant 35 × 45 mm photograph; travel medical insurance covering the requested visa period (or until Estonian public health insurance begins, if applicable); proof of purpose/admission; travel booking; biometric fingerprints; and financial evidence. Foreign public documents must be apostilled or legalised and translated into Estonian or English. The Embassy may request additional documents or an interview. Do not send original documents before receiving the Embassy's instructions.

4. Financial evidence — 2026 official amount and a published discrepancy.

The Estonian Ministry of Foreign Affairs' current general D-visa table lists €880 per month for studies (and €880/month for doctoral studies). The Estonian Academy of Arts' 2026 student guidance also confirms €880/month as the sufficient-funds benchmark. However, the Estonian Embassy New Delhi's Bangladesh/Nepal/Sri Lanka D-visa checklist still displays an older €800/month figure for studies. Treat €880/month as the current national 2026 planning benchmark, and obtain written confirmation from the Embassy about the evidence period and accepted financial documents before filing. The D-visa checklist asks for evidence of income for the three months immediately before application, showing amount, regularity and sources. This statutory sufficiency benchmark is not the full tuition or actual living-cost budget.

5. Health insurance and study TRP.

For the D visa, provide travel medical insurance for the requested visa validity unless Estonian Tervisekassa coverage will begin after arrival, in which case cover the gap until that insurance becomes valid. For the study TRP, provide a medical-expenses insurance contract meeting Estonian legal requirements for the permit period. Students who will stay for more than one year should apply for the study TRP through the Police and Border Guard Board (PBGB) service office in Estonia after arrival, following the university's instructions and booking an appointment. The European Commission describes applications at an Estonian foreign mission or, for a person already legally in Estonia, at PBGB; the Study in Estonia student guidance explains the usual D-visa-first, TRP-after-arrival sequence for longer degree programmes.

6. TRP duration and residence registration.

A study TRP is granted for the estimated/nominal study period subject to the statutory maximum (generally up to five years at a time) and may be extended where the student continues to meet the requirements. Register the place of residence in Estonia's Population Register within one month of arrival/receiving the permit as directed by the authority. Maintain full-time study progress; a student who interrupts studies or fails to meet the study requirements may lose the legal basis for the permit. The institution has sponsor obligations and must report relevant changes.

7. Work during study — no fixed student-hour cap, but study progress is essential.

A holder of a study TRP may work in Estonia without a separate work permit and without a fixed student-specific weekly hours cap, provided the employment does not interfere with studies and the student continues to meet the full-time study/progress requirements. Study in Estonia guidance says students must pass a full course load and finish within nominal time; university guidance commonly uses at least 75% of the annual study load (45 ECTS) as the progress test. This is not permission to neglect classes or guarantee of employment. Work must be legally contracted and taxed; undeclared work is unlawful and can create serious risks.

8. After graduation — 270 days (nine months).

After the study TRP expires, eligible graduates may remain in Estonia for up to 270 days (nine months) to seek a legal basis to extend their stay or apply for another residence permit, such as for employment. Study in Estonia also describes this as a nine-month job-search period. It is not automatic permission to start any job without meeting the relevant employment/residence rules; a suitable job and the appropriate work-based residence permission must be arranged before the post-study period ends. Confirm the graduate's exact expiry date and status with PBGB.

9. If studies are interrupted.

Under the Aliens Act, if a student interrupts studies or fails to complete the study programme to the extent required for the study TRP, the permit may expire 30 days after the interruption/failure. The student must contact the university and PBGB immediately if enrolment, course load or study progress changes; do not assume the full original permit remains valid.

10. Schengen travel distinction.

A valid Estonian D visa or residence permit permits short visits to other Schengen states under the usual 90 days in any 180-day period rule. It does not give the student a right to live, study long-term or work in another Schengen country.

Counsellor stop-check.

Verify the institution and programme recognition, admission and language requirement, New Delhi D-visa/TRP appointment, current €880/month funds test and three-month income evidence, apostille/legalisation and translation, travel and TRP health-insurance periods, visa-to-TRP sequence, one-month address registration, full-time study progress, 270-day graduate period and 30-day consequence of interruption. Never send a Sri Lankan client to the German Embassy Colombo for an Estonian D visa; that representation is for C visas only. Do not promise part-time work or guaranteed job placement.

Official references

  • Estonian Ministry of Foreign Affairs — Long-stay D visa, required documents and 2026 study funds (€880/month)
  • Estonian Embassy New Delhi — Sri Lankan applicants: C-visa representation, D visa/TRP appointment and submission instructions
  • Estonian Embassy New Delhi — D-visa checklist for Sri Lankan and other applicants (note older €800/month amount)
  • Study in Estonia — student D visa and TRP process for non-EU full-degree students
  • Study in Estonia — working during studies and nine-month post-graduation period
  • European Commission — Student in Estonia (permit, registration, work and post-study stay)
  • Estonian Aliens Act — study TRP, employment without separate permit and consequences of interrupted studies
  • Estonian Academy of Arts — 2026 €880/month funds guidance and study-progress obligations

Source owner: Estonian Ministry of Foreign Affairs / Embassy New Delhi (D visa and Sri Lanka route), Police and Border Guard Board and Estonian Aliens Act (TRP and legal conditions), Study in Estonia / Estonian Academy of Arts (student process, funds and study progress), European Commission (EU immigration overview). Re-audited 18 November 2026. Explicitly flagged: New Delhi Embassy checklist still states €800/month while the national MFA D-visa table and 2026 university guidance state €880/month; confirm the amount and evidence period with the Embassy before a live filing. The German Embassy in Colombo handles represented short-stay C visas only, not Estonian D visas.

ICELAND

Iceland — student residence permit, financial means, work and post-study route

Scope: non-EEA/EFTA applicants, including Sri Lankan students, intending to study at an eligible Icelandic university. Iceland is in the Schengen Area, but a student residence permit is a national Icelandic permit; it does not grant a right to work or reside in other Schengen states.

1. Confirm eligibility and full-time study.

The general student residence permit covers undergraduate, graduate and doctoral studies at an Icelandic university. The student must normally be at least 18 years old and enrolled full-time, defined as 30 ECTS per semester. Other eligible categories include recognised exchange programmes and certain technical/workplace studies; a private short course or language course is not automatically eligible. Obtain the institution's written confirmation that the exact programme is accepted as a basis for a student permit.

2. Apply early — paper application to the Directorate of Immigration.

The application, original supporting documents and payment receipt must be submitted in paper form to the Directorate of Immigration in Kópavogur, Iceland (by post or drop box). For the application to be received before the fall semester, the official deadline is 1 May; for the spring semester it is 1 October. These are latest receipt dates, not recommended posting dates. Prepare criminal-record and other documents well in advance. A complete application is essential; missing documents may delay or lead to refusal.

3. Financial evidence — independent funds required.

For applications received from 18 May 2026, the minimum secure means of support for one individual is ISK 259,951 per month (pre-tax reference amount). A 12-month example is ISK 3,119,412. Funds must cover the whole validity period. The student must generally demonstrate independent funds; a third-party sponsor is not automatically accepted. The stated exception is a legally resident marital spouse with sufficient funds to support both. Original bank confirmation, eligible scholarship/study loan or other specifically accepted evidence may be used. Do not treat a family sponsor letter alone as sufficient.

4. Health insurance and criminal record.

Submit health insurance valid in Iceland for at least three months from registration of legal domicile, with minimum coverage of ISK 2,000,000. After three months of registered legal domicile, the student is normally covered by Iceland's national health insurance. Submit a criminal-record certificate and any required translations/authentication in the form specified by the Directorate. The authority may request further evidence.

5. Fee and after-approval entry visa.

The student residence-permit application fee is ISK 70,000; renewal is also listed at ISK 70,000. The application is not processed without the fee receipt, and the fee is non-refundable if the applicant cancels. Once the residence permit is approved, if the student needs an entry visa, the Ministry sends a request for a D visa to the appropriate embassy. The official entry-visa list assigns Sri Lanka — Colombo, Royal Norwegian Embassy / VFS Global. This is the entry-visa issuance channel, not the place where the student residence-permit application itself is filed. Check current appointment/document arrangements with the Norwegian Embassy/VFS before travel.

6. Arrival formalities and permit validity.

A student permit may be granted for up to one year and renewed for up to one year at a time if conditions and academic progress are met. After approval/arrival, attend the residence-card photo appointment, submit the residence notification and complete a medical examination within two weeks if required. These steps must be completed within 90 days of permit approval or the permit may not be issued. If the holder stays outside Iceland for more than three consecutive months, the permit may be revoked; registering legal domicile outside Iceland automatically revokes it.

7. Academic progress and work rights — 2026 changes.

From the 2026 amendments, every renewal requires satisfactory academic progress, normally at least 75% of a full-time course load during the academic year. From the 2026 work-rights changes, holders of a valid student residence permit may work as employees without a separate work permit for up to 60% of full-time employment in total across all employers. During scheduled breaks between academic terms, full-time work is allowed. Self-employment remains prohibited. First-time applicants cannot begin working until their student residence permit has been issued. At renewal, employment information must be provided; exceeding the limit can affect the right to work.

8. Post-study job search — up to 18 months under the 2026 rules.

A foreign national who completes a Bachelor's, Master's or Doctoral degree at an Icelandic university may renew the student permit for up to 18 months after graduation to seek employment based on their specialist qualifications. This is a change effective in 2026; older pages or articles may still show the former three-year period. A doctoral graduate may also qualify for a separate specialist job-seeking permit for up to 12 months. These are application-based residence routes, not a guarantee of a job or automatic unrestricted work permission. Confirm the correct post-study category and current work conditions before the student graduates.

9. Family reunification.

Following the 2026 amendments, family reunification with a student is limited to holders enrolled in full-time university programmes leading to Bachelor's, Master's or Doctoral degrees. Diploma students are not eligible under this student-family route. Family applications are not processed at the same time or on an expedited basis as the student's application, so do not promise simultaneous approval or arrival.

Counsellor stop-check: verify eligible university/programme and 30 ECTS requirement; paper filing deadlines; original and authenticated documents; student's own funds (not a routine third-party sponsor); current ISK amount and 12-month calculation; ISK 2 million insurance; ISK 70,000 fee; Sri Lanka D-visa channel through Norwegian Embassy/VFS Colombo; post-arrival 90-day formalities; 75% study progress; 60% work cap; and 18-month post-study rule. Do not promise a job or claim that part-time income will satisfy the funds test.

Checked 18 November 2026. 2026 legislative amendments materially changed student work, renewal, family and post-study rules; do not rely on older university pages that still describe a separate work-permit requirement or a three-year post-study period.

USA

F-1 / M-1 student visa pathway — school, I-20, funds and work limits

Core distinction: the student visa is a non-immigrant travel document; the student's school admission and SEVIS record/Form I-20 establish the student-status pathway. F-1 is generally for academic and language study; M-1 is for vocational or other recognised non-academic study. A visitor visa is not a substitute for an F/M visa for a course leading to a U.S. degree or academic certificate.

1. Check the school and the exact programme first.

Confirm that the school is currently SEVP-certified for the relevant programme and can issue the correct Form I-20. Verify campus/location, programme level, start date, tuition and estimated living costs directly with the school's international student office. An admission offer alone is not an I-20 and does not prove visa eligibility.

2. Verify SEVIS registration and Form I-20.

After acceptance, the school enters the student's information in SEVIS and issues Form I-20. Check the student's legal name, date of birth, school/programme, SEVIS ID, start date, cost estimate and funding information. The student and school official must sign where required. Pay the applicable SEVIS I-901 fee and retain the receipt; confirm the current fee and exemptions on the official ICE/SEVP page before payment.

3. Prepare DS-160 and visa interview documents.

Complete the online DS-160 and retain the confirmation page. Follow the current U.S. Embassy Colombo appointment and document instructions. Core documents normally include a valid passport, DS-160 confirmation, fee receipt, photo if required, and signed Form I-20. The student should be ready to explain the course choice, academic preparation, why the chosen school fits, funding plan and plans after study. Consular officers may request transcripts, diplomas, test scores and other supporting evidence.

4. Funds — calculate from the school's I-20, not a made-up universal bank balance.

Build a documented funding plan for the tuition, living and travel costs shown by the school for the relevant study period. Match bank statements, sponsor income and relationship evidence, scholarship letters or approved loan documents to the I-20 cost estimate. Explain large recent deposits and the genuine source and availability of funds. Do not quote one fixed U.S. government minimum for every student; costs differ by school, location, programme and duration. Do not rely on hoped-for part-time earnings to fund the initial application.

5. Work rights — no guaranteed off-campus job.

Eligible F-1 students may generally work on campus up to 20 hours per week while school is in session; full-time on-campus work may be allowed during official breaks, subject to status and school rules. Off-campus work is restricted. Curricular Practical Training (CPT) requires the school's Designated School Official (DSO) authorisation and the correct I-20 endorsement before employment starts. Optional Practical Training (OPT) requires the applicable DSO recommendation and USCIS authorisation; the student must have the required Employment Authorization Document before starting where required. Do not tell a student they can freely work off campus or guarantee a job.

6. Travel and entry timing.

New F/M student visas may be issued up to 365 days before the programme start date, but a new student may not enter the United States more than 30 days before the start date. A visa does not guarantee admission: CBP makes the admission decision at the port of entry. Do not book non-refundable travel before visa issuance and confirm the current I-20 travel and arrival instructions with the DSO.

7. Counsellor stop-check.

Before advising, save the SEVP school/programme verification, admission letter, I-20 review, SEVIS fee receipt, DS-160 confirmation, current Colombo interview instructions, tuition/living-cost calculation, funding source documents, academic evidence, and any work-rights explanation given to the student. Escalate mismatched I-20 details, unexplained deposits, unclear sponsor funds, uncertain school certification or prior immigration/status issues. Never promise visa approval, employment or entry.
